Escalation (e.g., adding steroids or new biologics) is only warranted if both symptoms and objective inflammatory activity increase together. This curbs overtreatment risks, such as immunosuppression without benefit, and aligns with T2T and safety principles.\n\n### 5. Limits & Uncertainty\n\n- **Available Evidence:**  \n  - Full router plan (Type B, modules M4, M5, M9, M7A),\n  - Detailed patient timeline covering labs, meds, symptoms, and flares,\n  - Multi-snapshot diagnostic landscape,\n  - EoH Gold Standard suppression logic,\n  - Radiology/EHR ICU-analog patterns,\n  - ACR and EULAR guidelines,\n  - Research on persistent pain and PROs in RA.\n- **Not Available:**  \n  - Direct module outputs (e.g., numerical PSI, live suppression tables), real-time narrative/contextual stressor analysis, imaging for this episode, in-depth documentation of non-RA comorbidity.\n- **Uncertainty:**  \n  - If laboratory or functional measures are insensitive, mild or early inflammatory flares could be underestimated. However, given that objective/subjective discordance is explicitly described, and strong correlation between true flares and lab/function changes is robustly supported elsewhere in the same timeline and in ICU analogs, the choice of `noise_likely` is warranted.\n- **Type B tag rationale:**  \n  - The episode in question shows a clear decoupling of subjective symptoms from objective inflammation, and both EoH suppression logic and guideline/research backup argue against labeling this as a true RA flare.\n\n---\n\n**In summary:**  \nEoH flare-vs-noise logic, supported by both the patient’s concrete timeline and broader RA safety guidelines, finds that an episode of increased pain/fatigue without parallel objective changes is most consistent with “noise” (symbolic/fibro/psychosomatic), not a true inflammatory flare.\n\nTypeB_event_tag: noise_likely"}

event: phase_end

event: llm_done
data: {"text": "### 1. High-Signal Summary\n\nThis patient with early seropositive rheumatoid arthritis has experienced repeated moderate flares despite standard-dose oral methotrexate, as evidenced by a significant flare after missed doses (mid-2025) and a subsequent escalation to 20 mg weekly. After this adjustment, their symptoms, labs (CRP, ESR), and clinical assessments indicate disease control for the latter half of the year, with only minimal morning stiffness and near-normal labs. The diagnostic landscape remains strongly RA-like, and guidelines as well as EoH maintenance modules converge on a strategy of more active disease control for those with recent flare history, balancing the risks of overtreatment against preventing functional decline and further flares.\n\n### 2. Router-Aligned EoH Reasoning\n\n- **Step 1 (M1, M13): Assess Terrain, Flare Risk, and Trajectory**\n    - M1 would map this patient’s RA as currently controlled after a recent dose escalation, but with prior instability due to a confirmed flare following a methotrexate interruption. M13 would note that this creates a \"recent instability\" signal—flare risk over the next 12 months remains non-trivial, especially immediately after such a flare, though the normalization of labs and symptoms since the dose increase would gradually lower projected short-term risk.\n    - Timeline evidence includes: \n        - Early high disease activity with elevated CRP/ESR and 7/10 pain/stiffness,\n        - Improvement on initial methotrexate (15 mg), \n        - A moderate flare with high inflammatory markers in mid-2025 following missed doses,\n        - Flare control achieved with a prednisone taper and increase to methotrexate 20 mg,\n        - Stable labs and minimal symptoms after escalation for at least five months.\n\n- **Step 2 (M15, M22, M23): Adjust Therapy Intensity and Monitoring**\n    - M15 would propose a CarePlan maintaining the currently effective methotrexate dose, avoiding immediate taper or de-escalation, since recent instability dictates a conservative approach.\n    - M22 would advise keeping monitoring at the tighter (“higher”) end: regular clinical reviews (every 2–3 months), scheduled labs (CRP/ESR, blood counts, liver/renal function), and sustained emphasis on adherence, given proven high flare risk with any interruption.\n    - M23 rules would block any tapering (whether dose or monitoring frequency) until the patient demonstrates sustained, flare-free stability for at least 6–12 months—only after that could transition to lower-intensity monitoring or slower taper be considered.\n    - If instability recurred (flare, rising labs, increased symptoms), M23 auto-rollback logic would return to the more intensive prior treatment plan, as per EoH protocol.\n\n### 3. Evidence Answer (Guidelines, Research, Case-Analogs)\n\n- **Guideline Backbone**\n    - The ACR 2021 and EULAR 2022 guidelines both support escalation of therapy after failure of methotrexate monotherapy or repeated flares (even if due to non-adherence). Both strongly discourage taper until at least 6 months of documented low disease activity/remission.\n    - ACR specifically suggests maximizing methotrexate dose (oral or subcutaneous), switching to or combining with biologics or targeted synthetic DMARDs in persistent cases, and continuing regular lab surveillance and clinical monitoring.\n    - Tapering or maintenance reduction is only conditionally recommended after sustained target-level disease control for 6–12 months.\n\n- **Research / Trials**\n    - Observational and RCT data (per BeSt, Swefot, etc.) show flares are followed by increased risk of functional loss and joint damage, making repeat flares a trigger for therapy intensification—not reduction ([6], [8]). Long-term stability (not just short-term lab normalization) strongly predicts safe transitions to lower-intensity management.\n    - Studies (see [7], [15], [16]) reinforce that maximizing methotrexate (including consideration of parenteral route or folate adjustment for GI side effects) should precede DMARD switch or biologic initiation, and that monitoring intervals should remain close (> 3 monthly) in the first 6–12 months after any significant flare/instability.\n\n- **ICU/EHR Analog Notes**\n    - Not present in this context; timeline and guidelines are the primary evidence.\n\n#### ### Diagnostic Landscape Snapshot (Type C)\n- **2025-11-23 (current):**\n    - **RA-like**: dominant (0.79)\n    - **PsA-like**: minor (0.10)\n    - **Other**: minor/background (0.10)\n    - **SLE-like, Sjogren-like, Mixed CTD-like, Vasculitis-like**: not significant or absent\n    - Qualitatively, the RA phenotype is stable and central across all timepoints—no suggestion of major landscape drift.\n\n### 4. Safety Context\n\n- The guidelines emphasize the need to avoid excessive glucocorticoid use and not to step down DMARDs prematurely. Safety checks (labs, clinical exam, possibly imaging if new symptoms or damage concerns arise) remain core to ongoing care, particularly after any medication increase or recent flare. Methotrexate toxicity is monitored by standard protocols, and all DMARD reduction timelines are explicitly conditional on sustained disease control, in line with EoH and both ACR/EULAR recommendations.\n\n### 5. Limits & Uncertainty\n\n- **What Evidence Is Present:**\n    - Router plan (Type D, plan adjustment), full timeline of clinical/lab/medication events, multiple diagnostic landscape snapshots (with clear RA dominance), and up-to-date, relevant ACR/EULAR guideline excerpts.\n- **What Is Not Present:**\n    - No PSI or EoH-calibrated numeric risk scores (beyond explicit landscape weights).\n    - No imaging after the initial period, no failure of methotrexate at maximal tolerated dose, and no DMARD toxicity data.\n    - No data on patient preference or comorbidities, which may impact escalation/tapering decisions.\n- **Gaps:** \n    - Potential need for more granular monitoring of adherence and of adverse events (none detailed here).\n    - No detailed discussion of next escalation steps if another flare occurs (e.g., addition of biologic/tsDMARD), but guidelines and EoH logic imply that would be considered next.\n- **Uncertainty:**\n    - The appropriateness of down-titrating either therapy or monitoring frequency is contingent on continued stability without further flares. If non-adherence or GI side effects persist, subcutaneous methotrexate or alternative DMARD strategies (per guidelines) may be preferred.\n    - All plan adjustments should be revisited in real-time if there are further significant symptoms or lab changes.\n\n---\n\n**In summary:**\nEoH would recommend maintaining the current (higher) methotrexate dose and NOT reducing therapy or monitoring intensity in the next 6–12 months, given the patient’s recent moderate flare and now-improved but still provisional control. Monitoring should remain at the \"closer\" end of typical follow-up (every 2–3 months), with regular labs and focus on adherence. Only after at least 6 months of documented clinical and laboratory stability should stepwise reductions in monitoring or therapy be considered, as this best balances flare risk reduction with avoidance of overtreatment."}

Router-Aligned EoH Reasoning\n\n- **Step 1 (M50, M48C): Analyze and explain diagnostic landscape weights**\n  - Module 50 constructs a structured diagnostic landscape using concrete EoH facts. Here, the latest snapshot (document [4]) shows SLE-like as the top weight (0.7), MCTD-like next (0.2), and a minor RA-like element (0.05). data: {"text": "### 1. High-Signal Summary\n\nFollowing the router plan (Type D: plan adjustment), Ethos-of-Health would structure the care plan for this psoriatic arthritis (PsA) patient–with both axial and peripheral involvement–by analyzing trajectory and control using observed timeline events, the diagnostic landscape, and current guideline evidence. The timeline shows initial inadequate NSAID response, then clear symptomatic and serologic improvement (CRP from 1.8 to 0.4 mg/dL) after transition to adalimumab, with minimal pain and disease features well controlled in recent months. The diagnostic landscape has become steadily more \"psa_like\"-dominant, supporting high confidence in the main label and suggesting the current regimen is effective. However, risk of flare and radiographic progression persists due to underlying axial and entheseal features.\n\n### 2. Router-Aligned EoH Reasoning\n\n- **Step 1 (M1, M2, M3A): Terrain & Historical Pattern Assessment**\n  - These modules would use the timeline to map the patient’s starting terrain: longstanding psoriasis, new asymmetric arthritis, dactylitis, enthesitis, and rapid climb in \"psa_like\" diagnostic weight as symptoms and HLA-B27 status became clear.\n  - Patterns: an initial symptomatic phase, suboptimal NSAID response, then stabilization post-TNF inhibitor (adalimumab).\n  - The modules would flag high baseline risk bands due to mixed axial/peripheral involvement but recognize a shift toward lower stack levels and improved stability in the months on biologic therapy.\n\n- **Step 2 (M13, M14): Forecast Flare Risk and Trajectory**\n  - Flare risk and frequency would be conceptually projected using the trend toward disease control on adalimumab, minus evidence of loss-of-response or subclinical drift. \n  - M13 would give extra weight to persistently normal CRP, absence of new synovitis/enthesitis, and minimal pain in the last review.\n  - Ongoing elevated risk would remain due to combined axial + peripheral phenotype and history of enthesitis/dactylitis, both markers for more severe and fluctuating disease.\n\n- **Step 3 (M15, M22, M23): Care Plan Adjustment**\n  - M15 would synthesize this into a care plan that *maintains* current biologic intensity, as rapid taper or de-escalation risks flare and radiographic progression, per guideline themes.\n  - Visits and labs: The system would recommend moderate to high-frequency monitoring, particularly in the first 6–12 months after any regimen change, *then* gradual de-escalation if remission is sustained.\n  - Monitoring bands: Regular CRP (every 2–3 months initially), routine symptom/functional assessment, periodic musculoskeletal imaging if axial symptoms change.\n  - Adjustments: If sustained remission (>6 months), M15 might consider spacing of biologic dosing/visits, but only with explicit shared decision-making and careful watch for subclinical drift.\n\n### 3. Evidence Answer (Guidelines, Research, Case-Analogs)\n\n**Guideline Backbone:**\n- *EULAR 2019/2020 and ASAS-EULAR 2022 AxSpA recommendations* [Refs 11, 13, 14, 15]:  \n  - Stress individualized management with both pharmacologic and non-pharmacologic therapies, with “tight control” of inflammation as a core principle.\n  - Axial + peripheral involvement → biologic DMARD (TNFi, IL-17i, JAKi) is indicated when NSAIDs/csDMARDs are insufficient.\n  - Sustained remission is required for DMARD taper; otherwise, maintenance at current intensity advised for at least 12 months.\n  - Visit and lab cadence: individualized, but guidelines support every 3 months during adjustment and every 3–6 months once stable.\n\n- *ACR/NPF 2018 PsA guideline* [Refs 9, 12]:  \n  - Emphasizes early escalation for aggressive/axial disease, close monitoring for functional or radiographic progression, and combination of clinical and biomarker review.\n\n**Research Cohorts & Trials:**  \n- TICOPA and related “treat-to-target” studies [Ref 11] highlight benefits of close monitoring (monthly/quarterly visits, regular CRP) in reducing flares and joint damage.\n- Multiple RCTs (e.g., adalimumab, certolizumab, secukinumab) [Ref 9]: support ongoing biologic therapy in patients with both axial and peripheral PsA, especially if initial response is robust.\n\n**Case Analogs:**  \n- EoH would treat this patient as having transitioned from high risk (active disease, frequent flares) to a newly stabilized phase, but, as seen in cohort studies, risk of flare and progression remains higher in patients with axial/enthesitis history.\n\n---\n\n#### ### Diagnostic Landscape Snapshot (Type C)\n- **psa_like:** dominant (0.8, high confidence, current snapshot and recent months)\n- **ra_like:** minor background (0.1, slight increase, likely due to some joint overlap but not dominant)\n- **sle_like, sjogren_like, mixed_ctd_like, vasculitis_like:** absent (0.0)\n- **other:** minor (0.1, decreased sharply since early timeline)\n  - The landscape has evolved from other/minimal (pre-diagnosis) to a clear and stable “psa_like” dominance since starting adalimumab.\n\n### 4. Safety Context\n\n- Guidelines emphasize avoiding premature DMARD taper, especially within the first sustained remission period, and advocate for regular safety monitoring (CBC, LFTs, infection risk).\n- Systemic glucocorticoids discouraged long-term; NSAIDs can be continued for persistent axial symptoms if tolerated.\n- Education and shared decision-making, with encouragement for regular exercise and risk modification (smoking cessation, etc.), are emphasized by EULAR/ASAS.\n\n### 5. Limits & Uncertainty\n\n- **What’s present:**  \n  - Router plan (Type D, modules M1/M13/M15, document handles aligned),\n  - Full synthetic timeline with dated events for diagnosis, flares, treatment response (NSAIDs, adalimumab),\n  - Serial diagnostic landscape snapshots showing a rise and stabilization in “psa_like” weight and high-confidence labeling,\n  - Research/guideline excerpts (EULAR, ASAS, ACR/NPF, tight control studies).\n\n- **What’s absent or partial:**  \n  - No direct PSI or stack-level numbers, but module function is clear.\n  - No patient-reported outcomes, imaging details, or full EHR data.\n  - Guideline summaries are high-level; specific visit/lab frequency is not numerically prescribed.\n  - Radiographic progression assessment is inferred, not directly measured.\n\n- Based on the clinical context and the EoH model, the system would favor *maintaining* current TNFi therapy, moderate-to-high visit and lab cadence (closer to every 2–3 months initially), and only reconsidering taper after a full 6–12 months of thoroughly documented remission, with restoration to higher intensity if any subclinical drift, flare, or new symptoms arise.  \n- The primary limitation is lack of calibrated numeric flare risk or explicit PSI scores; all reasoning is anchored in observed events, guideline recommendations, and conceptual module outputs.\n\n---\n\n**In summary:**  \nEoH would recommend sustained biologic therapy (adalimumab or equivalent), moderate-to-high monitoring intensity (visits/labs every 2–3 months), and maintenance in the current stability band, with only careful, shared-decision taper after protracted remission–all justified by timeline control, diagnostic landscape, and guideline backbone."}

This patient's timeline reveals persistent dryness and fatigue (chronic baseline), supported by seropositivity (anti-SSA/SSB, ANA), plus periods of symptomatic improvement on pilocarpine with ongoing moderate fatigue. There is no clear documentation of systemic (extraglandular) flare episodes in the timeline, but the evolution of the diagnostic landscape shows strong and consistent “sjogren_like” dominance with minimal noise from other overlap syndromes, suggesting calibration is favoring recognition of true baseline without overshooting toward new acute diagnoses.\n\n### 2. Router-Aligned EoH Reasoning\n\n- **Step 1: Global Calibration and Suppression Governance (M48, M48B)**\n  - M48 and M48B would conceptually synthesize timeline events, symptom patterns, and objective markers into stability bands and stack levels, using suppression logic to avoid false-positive flare detection (per EoH v2, page 7/10).\n  - In this timeline, background sicca symptoms (dry eyes/mouth) are persistent, but there are no distinct spikes or abruptly worsening systemic features documented that would typically trigger an acute flare detection or band escalation.\n  - Chronic fatigue persists, but with some improvement in dryness after pilocarpine, suggesting the model would likely keep the patient in a compensated chronic baseline, avoiding unnecessary alerts.\n  - Suppression logic (see EoH pseudocode excerpt) specifically filters out symbolic, psychosomatic, or lab-artifact events—here, it would be expected not to flag routine fatigue or dryness as a flare.\n\n- **Step 2: Diagnostic Landscape Stability and Drift (M48C)**\n  - M48C reviews diagnostic landscape history to identify drift or inappropriate label switching that might indicate over- or under-detection.\n  - Across multiple timepoints (late 2024, mid-2025, late 2025), “sjogren_like” maintains clear top weight (from ~0.56 up to 0.91, then 0.8), with “other” and “sle_like” only minimally represented.\n  - The progression toward greater “sjogren_like” confidence, then slight relaxation back to 0.8, suggests both stability and an absence of spurious flare labeling (i.e., no sudden “sle_like” or “vasculitis_like” dominance).\n  - There is no signal of diagnostic instability or inappropriate label drift over time, which would argue against under- or over-suppression.\n\n### 3. data: {"text": "### 1. High-Signal Summary\n\nFollowing the router plan for Type C (explainability/diagnostic landscape) using modules M48C and M50, we analyze a patient with evolving mixed connective tissue disease (MCTD) features over a visible timeline. Early events included Raynaud phenomenon, positive ANA, and high anti-U1 RNP, leading to an initial landscape dominated by MCTD. During a flare with myositis and elevated CK, MCTD remained top-weight, but by the most recent snapshot, the EoH diagnostic landscape had shifted—weights redistributed, with RA-like now leading and SLE-like also present. This pattern shows landscape drift rather than long-term stability at a single dominant label.\n\n### 2. Router-Aligned EoH Reasoning\n\n- **Step 1 (M48C, M50): Landscape Evolution and Stability/Drift**\n    - The router plan directs Module M48C to audit for stability/drift and M50 to assemble structured diagnostic landscapes from episodes of health.\n    - *Timeline anchoring*: Initial events (Oct 2024) – Raynaud, arthralgias, high ANA (1:1280), and anti-U1 RNP (200 U/mL) → landscape dominated by \"mixed_ctd_like\" (weight ~0.77), top label with high confidence.\n    - *Flare event (April 2025)*: Severe myositis (CK 900 U/L, myalgias, dyspnea) – further increases the mixed_ctd_like dominance (weight rising to ~0.83).\n    - *Follow-up (late 2025)*: After treatment and symptom control, diagnostic landscape shifts—now \"ra_like\" leads (0.5), \"sle_like\" secondary (0.33), mixed_ctd_like drops out, and \"other\" remains as minor component; entropy and normalized entropy increase, showing greater diagnostic spread.\n    - A contemporaneous landscape snapshot (from the timeline) during MCTD care gives \"mixed_ctd_like\" still top but with visible \"ra_like\" (0.15) and \"sle_like\" (0.10) subweights. This supports a gradual move from MCTD-pure toward a mixed/RA-overlap phenotype.\n\n- **M48C Drift Audit**  \n    - The pattern from these snapshots—especially the discrete jump from dominant mixed_ctd_like to a split RA- and SLE-like—exemplifies diagnostic drift rather than strict phenotype stability.\n\n- **M50 Structured Landscape Output**  \n    - Per policy, M50 treats episodes with no dominant candidate as \"top-3\" outputs, providing a short ranked list when case complexity or ambiguity rises (e.g., mixed/overlap states).\n    - Provenance: Each candidate’s weight is explicitly linked to clinical features: initial Raynaud and anti-RNP for MCTD; evolving arthritis and myositis for RA/SLE components; chronicity of muscle involvement may have influenced rise in SLE-like.\n    - Evidence items would cite labs (ANA, RNP, CK), symptoms (Raynaud, myalgias), and treatment response (hydroxychloroquine, mycophenolate).\n  \n### 3. data: {"text": "### 1. High-Signal Summary (2–4 sentences)\n\nBased on multidisciplinary guideline and research data retrieved here, the long-term non-pharmacologic management of chronic widespread pain consistent with fibromyalgia emphasizes patient self-management, regular low-impact exercise, structured sleep hygiene, and evidence-backed cognitive-behavioral or mindfulness-based therapies. Guideline excerpts, VA/DoD patient guides, and systematic reviews collectively support these behavioral strategies as core foundational treatments, often producing improvements in function and quality of life. Pacing daily activities, goal-setting, and multimodal, interdisciplinary care are repeatedly highlighted. These themes align closely with the VA and CDC recommendations for chronic pain and are also reflected in published systematic reviews and protocol studies.\n\n### 2. Router-Aligned EoH Reasoning\n\n- The router plan classified this as a **Type D (Plan adjustment)** question: focus is placed squarely on guideline-driven strategies, with Ethos-of-Health flare/tiering concepts not required.\n- Key principles extracted from the patient’s timeline (low inflammatory markers, diagnosis of fibromyalgia, initiation of duloxetine, improvement noted with sleep routine and exercise) are in line with major guideline themes for chronic pain/fibromyalgia:\n  - **Emphasis on self-management:** Guidelines (see VA/DoD and CDC patient pain guides) frame self-management as the “basis for managing your day,” stressing patient control over activity, rest, and coping decisions.\n  - **Exercise interventions:** The timeline highlights physician-advised “graded exercise,” reinforced by research and guidelines which recommend individualized, progressive exercise (aerobic, stretching, gentle resistance, yoga, tai chi) as a key element in pain and function improvement.\n  - **Behavioral therapies:** Guidelines recommend cognitive-behavioral therapy (CBT), mindfulness-based cognitive therapy (MBCT), behavioral activation (BA), acceptance and commitment therapy (ACT), and related approaches; these are recognized to help modify pain perceptions, manage distress, and improve coping.\n  - **Pacing and activity planning:** Planning and breaking up daily tasks, incorporating rest periods, and goal-setting are repeatedly recommended for pain control and function maintenance.\n  - **Sleep hygiene:** A consistent bedtime and sleep rituals, as observed in the timeline, are backed by guidelines to help mitigate non-restorative sleep—one of the most disabling aspects of fibromyalgia.\n- Research reviews (such as PMC8284796: Self-management for chronic widespread pain) report that structured self-management programs—including these nonpharmacologic/behavioral elements—show improvements in physical function and sometimes in pain/disability.\n- Interdisciplinary and multimodal care: Guidelines encourage a team-based approach, especially for patients with functional impairment, overlapping depression/anxiety, or psychosocial vulnerabilities.\n\n### 3. Evidence Answer (Guidelines, Research, Case-Analogs)\n\n**Guideline backbone (clearly supported in context):**\n- **VA/DoD Guidelines (see Chronic Pain Management: A Patient’s Guide; Vadodopioidscpgpatientsummary):**\n  - “Self-management is the basis for managing your day and making sure that other treatments work as well as possible.”\n  - Non-medication treatments such as physical therapy, yoga, acupuncture, and behavioral therapies are specifically recommended.\n  - Behavioral approaches, including CBT, ACT, BA, and MBCT, are recommended for helping patients adapt their response to chronic pain and improve function.\n  - Interdisciplinary care with an integrated team is suggested for patients with complex needs.\n- **Research evidence:**\n  - **Systematic Review (PMC8284796):** Self-management programs (including exercise, psychological strategies, pacing, sleep improvement) consistently improve physical function over both short and long term for chronic widespread pain/fibromyalgia.\n  - **Exercise review (PMC7773269):** Group-based and individual exercise are effective, with no significant differences versus other non-pharmacologic interventions for chronic pain; exercise remains a mainstay recommendation.\n- **Additional strategies referenced in guidelines:**\n  - Mindfulness, meditation, and biofeedback as complementary strategies.\n  - “Breaking up daily activities into shorter periods with breaks to not worsen your pain.”\n\n**Case-analogs:** No ICU/EHR case analogs were retrieved for chronic pain/fibromyalgia.\n\n### Diagnostic Landscape Snapshot (Type C)\n\n_Not required by the router plan for this question. However, the diagnostic landscape for this patient is fully dominated by “other” (fibromyalgia/non-inflammatory) labels, with 0.5 weight assigned to \"other\" and much lower or zero for inflammatory (RA-like, SLE-like, etc.) conditions, reinforcing guideline-congruent management above._\n\n### 4. Safety Context\n\nThe guidelines emphasize non-pharmacologic, multimodal strategies as first-line for chronic pain and fibromyalgia—highlighting reduced risk of medication side effects, especially compared to opioids or polypharmacy. A focus on patient empowerment, pacing, and integrated behavioral health is designed to promote safety and function, reduce addiction risk, and avoid unnecessary escalation to pharmacologic therapies.\n\n### 5. Limits & Uncertainty\n\n- **Evidence present:** Multiple guideline excerpts (VA/DoD pain management, patient guides), systematic reviews (PubMed), general research protocols, and the patient’s timeline (diagnosis, labs, sleep/exercise interventions).\n- **Evidence not present:** There are no direct EoH flare/tier values, no risk stratification tables, and no ICU/EHR case analogs specific to fibromyalgia in this retrieval.\n- **Key limitations:** The guideline excerpts are general and thematic—not providing granular exercise prescriptions, detailed CBT module breakdowns, or individualized pacing protocols. No direct comparison of individual behavioral therapies (CBT vs. ACT, for example) is provided.\n- **Summary of certainty:** The recommendations above are widely endorsed across VA/DoD and CDC guidelines, and are strongly supported by systematic review evidence. While patient trajectory improvement is observed in the timeline, quantifying effect size or long-term risk/benefit is outside the scope of the retrieved data.\n\n**In conclusion:** Long-term, guideline-based management for fibromyalgia focuses on patient self-management, individualized exercise, sleep hygiene, and structured behavioral therapies (CBT, mindfulness, pacing), with interdisciplinary support where needed. These strategies are robustly supported across guideline and research sources in this retrieval."}
